A pocket router, or sometimes called a travel router, is a little box with Wi-Fi and Ethernet that takes one internet connection and turns it into a private Wi-Fi network for all of your gear. Now, that one connection could be the hotel Wi-Fi, a wired Ethernet jack in the room, or tethering to your phone over USB, or even a USB cellular modem if you've got one of those. You log the router into the hotel or cruise ship Wi-Fi once except the captive portal once and then all your phones, tablets, laptops, Apple TV, Steam Deck, and everything else just sees your Wi-Fi, not the hotels. To the hotel, it all looks like one single device. And that solves a bunch of real problems. Places that charge per device suddenly seem a lot more reasonable, and places that limit the number of devices suddenly aren't a limitation. Captive portals that don't work at all on Apple TV or a game console suddenly become a non-issue because only the router needs to actually talk to them. You avoid the flaky hotel Wi-Fi configurations. You get to use your own network name and your own password. And you can put everything behind one VPN without configuring VPN on every single device individually. So if your family has a half a dozen phones, laptops, and tablets, they all just remember your router and suddenly everybody is connected as soon as the router is. So that's the basic idea. Now, let me show you how that paid off in the wild. This weekend, I was at a big car show at a convention center with my Pontiac. Like a lot of modern venues, the only Wi-Fi they offered to the general public was sponsored. You know the type. You connect to the SSID, get dumped into their captive portal, watch an unskippable video ad about some nonsense, click through a couple more pages, agree to some terms, and then you get a tiny slice of internet time and bandwidth. Go to the bathroom once, and you have to do it all over again. Sometimes that's annoying enough if you have a single device, but if you've got a phone, a tablet, and maybe a laptop in the booth, it gets old fast. I plugged that pillar Ethernet into the WAN port on the router. Then I plugged a USB battery pack into the router's USBC port to power it because, as I said, there was no AC outlet anywhere nearby. But in about a minute, I had a private Wi-Fi hotspot centered around my car. And because I control the SSID, I called it free Pontiac Wi-Fi. Once you understand how to use a pocket router, then hotels, convention centers, Airbnbs, cruise ships, even airplanes all start to look like different flavors of just WAN input that you can bend to your will. So, let's talk about how to use a pocket router when you travel step by step. And the first rule of Pocket Router Club is do the initial setup at home. And today, we're going to venture into the world of modern memory architecture, but with a twist. And then I'll share some comparison benchmarks towards the end once we understand the platforms better. On the one side, we've got the sleek, streamlined M2 Ultra Mac Pro from Apple, featuring 128 GB of what Apple calls unified memory. On the other, we've got GMK Tech Nookbox, a sleek 16 core Ryzen desktop equipped with the AMD 8060S APU, also with integrated graphics, but running on a shared DDR5 system. They both use integrated graphics. They can both edit video. They both run productivity apps, but beyond those surface similarities, their memory systems may as well come from two different planets, and that's what we're going to explore today. Apple's M2 Ultra and their Pro systems, and indeed, most of Apple silicon is built around something called a unified memory architecture, or UMA. The M2 Ultra is a behemoth of a chip. Up to 24 cores, 60 GPU cores, and a neural engine to boot. But what sets it apart isn't just what's on the chip. It's how the memory is arranged around it. Instead of slapping in some sodiums on a motherboard and calling it a day, Apple took the bold step of integrating the memory directly onto the chip package using a silicon interposer. That means the LPDDR5 memory modules aren't somewhere off in the weeds or on the bus. They're right next to the SoC. And not in a close enough kind of way, but in a shared substrate with a thousand pin connection kind of way. We're talking about a 1024bit memory bus capable of delivering up to 800 GB per second of bandwidth. That's not a typo. 800 gigabytes per second. So, what does all the extra bandwidth and proximity actually buy you? Well, in Apple's, all the components, the CPU, the GPU, the NPU, and even the image signal processor share access to the same pool of memory in a cache coherent fashion. And this is a big deal because on a traditional PC, things aren't nearly as cooperative. Enter the AMD 860S in the GMK Tech Nookbox. The 8060S lives inside a Ryzen APU, and like many of its x86 siblings, it runs in what's called a shared memory model. But because the memory bus is narrower, the total bandwidth is limited to somewhere in the 80 GB range, depending on configuration. Not bad, but once again, about 1/8 of what the M2 Ultra can do. ## 41. WinFS: Windows Future Storage - Canceled!  What You Need to Know from a Retired Microsoft Engineer
**Channel:** Dave's Garage | **Views:** 147K | **Date:** 9 months ago | **Duration:** 14:33 | **ID:** h5d5H92c4Mk
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=h5d5H92c4Mk

### Transcript:
Hey, I'm Dave. Welcome to my shop. I'm Dave Plameumber, a retired operating systems engineer going back to the MSTOS and Windows 95 days. And today, we're exploring the history of one of Microsoft's most famous missteps, WinFS. Full disclosure, I was there on the NT team at the time, but I wasn't personally working on it. Our shell would support it, but my team was just a consumer of WinFS. But even that begs the question, what was WinFS? Well, let me set the stage. Back in the early 2000s, a new problem was emerging for PC users. The proliferation of digital devices like personal computers, cameras, and the newly nent smartphone market had unleashed an avalanche of data upon users. Documents multiplied, photographs accumulated, and emails poured into inboxes with relentless regularity. Hard drives once normally measured in megabytes now boasted capacity sometimes into the gigabytes. Yet, the tools to manage this burgeoning digital bounty remained stubbornly rooted in the past. This data deluge was compounded by the rise of the internet which introduced new types of content like MP3s, digital videos, and web archives, further straining traditional file management systems. For example, a 2003 study estimated that the average PC user was then managing over 10,000 files, a number that was doubling every few years. The traditional file system, a hierarchical structure of folders and files, had served this admirably in the simpler days of computing. However, by the year 2000, its limitations were becoming painfully evident. Users found themselves mired in a web of directories, searching futilely for a misplaced document or struggling to recall the cryptic naming conventions they had developed a year prior. After all, a lot of people's conception of data management involved saving all their files to the desktop and then maybe once a year pushing them down into a new folder they create called old desktop, just like they did last year and the year before. The system back then offered no insight into the relationships between your files. There was no way to connect a photograph to the event it captured or an email to the project that it was about. It was a method of storage and not much different from a digital shoe box at times. Microsoft stepped into this fray as the company perceived not merely a problem but also an opportunity. A chance to redefine how people interacted with their digital possessions. And thus was born WinFS or Windows future storage. a project of such audacity that it sought to bridge the chasm between the file system and the database, promising a future where data was not merely stored but also understood. The name WinFS itself was a nod to its forward-looking ambitions with future storage signaling Microsoft's intent to leap beyond the constraints of existing file systems like FAT32 and NTFS. Microsoft's ambition with WinFS was nothing short of revolutionary. The company envisioned a storage system that transcended the rudimentary capabilities of the predecessors. Where traditional file systems had treated data as isolated entities, simple files strewn across the digital landscape of your hard drive with little regard for their meaning, when FS attempted to imbue them with structure and context. It proposed to transform files into items, each enriched with metadata capable of forming relationships with other items, much like entries in a relational database. The whole idea was partly inspired by earlier research projects like the Cairo operating system, a 1990s Microsoft initiative that aimed to integrate object-oriented storage, but was shelved due to its complexity. When FS was in some ways Cairo's spiritual successor, carrying forward the dream of a smarter file system. This concept drew inspiration from the world of enterprise computing where relational databases had long proven their worth. In such systems, data is organized into tables with rows and columns delineating records and their attributes. In a relational database, as you might have guessed, relationships can also be defined between columns and between tables. A user might query the database for all customers in a specific region or all transactions exceeding a certain amount, and the system would respond with precision. Microsoft sought to bring this power to the desktop, enabling users to ask sophisticated questions of their own data, like, "Show me all the photographs from my 2003 vacations or list every document related to the quarterly report." Now, this was long before AI, so the system wasn't going to figure it out on its own. So to achieve it, WinFS introduced the notion of schemas. Templates that define the properties of the various data types. A schema for a contact might include fields for name, address, and telephone number, while one for a document might specify the author, date, and keywords. ultimately winfs never made it into a production Windows release it was a case of an idea ahead of its time brilliant but impractical to ship back in 2006 another feature NextGen secure Computing also known as Palladium which was an ambitious security initiative was also dropped from the release road map Microsoft had to be ruthless only including features that could be realistically completed and stabilized in the new time frame essentially Longhorn had to be res scoped to something achievable and so the team regrouped they took Windows Server 2003 sp1 which was a solid reliable version of Windows and used that as the new core for Longhorn and from there they began reintegrating those salvageable Parts this time they applied the lessons learned the development was much more disciplined features like Avalon the new graphics UI stack and indigo for communications were still in because they were far enough along and important enough the new new desktop Window Manager enabling those Arrow effects was also retained but now everything was built on a no unstable base instead of that shaky pre-retcon being followed client windows and server windows were unified on the same core going forward no more Divergence where the server is stabled and the client is a wild experiment they would continue to share the same fundamentals and indeed ever since that reset Microsoft has kept client and server Windows unlock step on a single code base even if the releases don't alow which is a huge positive Legacy of this Saga the development after 2004 was essentially a Mad Dash to get the new Longhorn now truly Windows version 6 ready to ship by mid 2005 Microsoft felt confident enough to give it an official product name Windows Vista the word Vista was meant to evoke a beautiful view a fresh perspective probably to distance itself from the negativity surrounding LongHorn's delays as Vista took shape it became clear that it would deliver some of LongHorn's promise but not all so what made it and what didn't well Vista what was once to be Longhorn did ship with a lot of visible enhancements the arrow gooey made it the prime time Vista introduced the translucent glass window frames smooth animations and a new start menu with integrated search the sidebar with gadgets also appeared you could have a clock weather RSS feed and so on on your desktop sidebar a direct descendant of the longhorn sidebar concept the instant search capability was present through the Explorer and start menu thanks to an indexing service now this wasn't win s the files were still on NTFS but you had a fast index to search files and even their file metadata which delivered some of the user experience that winfs had aimed for in Vista if you hit the start button and start typing results pop up almost instantly and that was new and it felt pretty magical compared to the old Windows XP search dog you do remember the little search dog helper right Vista also brought in the Avalon Graphics infrastructure as the windows presentation Foundation or WPF even if the OS itself only used WPF in a few places like parts of the media Center app and certain system apps Indigo shipped as well renamed to WCF enabling modern ways for applications to communicate over networks these were delivered as part of The NET Framework 3.0 that came with Vista essentially fulfilling the plan of integrating those net pillars into Windows on the security front the lessons of 2003 to 2004 were deeply ingrained in Vista it shipped with user account control a feature that while annoying to many users initially was a direct result of the new security mindset even administrators would run with limited privileges most of the time to prevent malware or accidental changes from Wrecking the system internally Vista had been engineered with a much more rigorous security development life cycle meaning that more threat modeling code reviews and testing for vulnerabilities was done than earlier windows so in many ways Vista was more solid under the hood than even XP had been even if it had got a reputation for being a bit heavy that said vista's launch in late 2006 wasn't all smooth sailing although the development did coales and Vista shipped it arrived with a bit of a mixed reception many of you probably remember the stories or experienced it yourself Vista was reasonably resource heavy and if you tried to run it on a machine that was fine for XP but not much more you were likely in for a bad time it really needed a decent GPU for the arrow feature and more RAM than was common on PC sold just a few years prior some drivers weren't ready or were unstable because Hardware vendors had trouble keeping up with the changes the graphics driver model in particular had changed significantly to accommodate the new desktop window manager so a lot of people who upgr early on had issues with devices not working or with system crashes the Press was pretty harsh about vista's problems and it gained a bit of an unfair stigma I say unfair because with a few updates and on proper Hardware Vista was actually a pretty solid OS but first impressions tend to stick nonetheless Microsoft had delivered what Longhorn became it was no longer vaporware or endless beta Vista was real for better or worse now stepping back you might wonder was the longhorn project a failure well yes and no it failed to deliver on its original promises when a Fest never shipped and many of the more radical ideas were cut and the schedule slipped dramatically after all Vista arrived more than 5 years after XP whereas originally Longhorn was thought of as a three-year or less Gap it was also something of a management failure and that it took a near crisis for Microsoft to course correct but LongHorn's Legacy is complex and in many ways positive for one the reset and recovery from Longhorn shaped Microsoft's culture and Engineering practices going forward the fact that they eventually pulled a workable Vista out of the disastrous Longhorn effort is a testament to the company's ability to learn and adapt albeit painfully post Vista the windows team adopted more disciplined planning the very next release Windows 7 which came in 2009 was essentially Vista done right and it polished and optimized everything that Vista introduced and it was a huge success without The Crucible of Longhorn Vista Windows 7 might not have been as good as it was in fact a lot of folks who skipped Vista and went straight to Windows 7 were indirectly enjoying the fruits of LongHorn's ambitious features just in more refined form today whether on Windows 10 11 Mac OS or even your smart phone you expect to hit a search box and find files or information reasonably instantly win ifs's Grand Vision didn't materialize but it definitely push the envelope on what users would come to expect Microsoft ended up building a robust indexing service into Windows first in Vista and improved in later versions and introduced the idea of libraries in Windows 7 libraries are virtual folders aggregating cont content type something like what wiest's promise of organizing by metadata would have done on the developer side The NET Framework became a core part of Windows development Vista ship. net 3.0 and since then net has grown by Leaps and Bounds and today we have net 9 a huge ecosystem of apps and tools built upon it the idea of using manage code for safety and productivity is now pretty mainstream at Microsoft consider that Microsoft's latest Frameworks and even parts of Windows 10 like the new Settings app parts of the new start menu and so on use net under the hood Longhorn was the inflection point where net moved from a fringe developer option to a core Windows technology another Legacy the emphasis on security from the longhorn reset era stuck modern Windows starting with Vista has a security architecture that's much more robust UAC driver signing sandboxing features and so on all took root in that time frame while users grumbled about permission prompts in hindsight that was a necessary Evolution if Longhorn hadn't hit the wall maybe those security lessons would have come even later with even more costly consequences during the age of internet connectivity and perhaps the biggest lesson Longhorn taught was about project management and focus you can have the best engineers in the world and Microsoft had plenty of brilliant people on Longhorn but if the project lacks clear Focus or if priorities keep shifting and if you're trying to do too much at once you can easily get a big mess instead of a great product after Longhorn Vista Microsoft became a lot more careful about not letting Windows projects get so unwieldly the cycle of huge monolithic Windows releases gave way to a more incremental approach and eventually the windows is a service model in Windows 10 it's ironic because even though Longhorn aim to revolutionize everything in one go the way it actually influenced Windows was by prompting it to a more evolutionary Improvement in the future just the way I like it from a personal perspective as somebody who lived through some of the longhorn Saga from the inside and watch the rest from the outside it's a fascinating cautionary tale it shows that even a company with virtually Limitless resources and a track record of shipping big products can get in over its head the story of Longhorn is one of huus and humility Microsoft reached for the Stars stumbled but then humbled itself to regroup and ultimately deliver something worthwhile and many of the Innovations did survive in some form it just took a couple of releases and several more years to fully B them so that's the story of Longhorn in a nutshell a dream of a revolutionary OS an epic struggle to deliver it and a dramatic reset and then ultimately the birth of Windows Vista a scarred but significant milestone in the windows lineage it's a story of overreaching by also of learning and recovery and it left an imprint on all windows releases that followed if you found this sneak peek behind the scenes informative or interesting please remember
